# An Introduction to Design and Culture An Introduction to Design and Culture provides students with an comprehensive guide to the <sup></sup>anging relationship between design and culture from 1900 to the present day, with an emphasis on <sup>fi</sup>ve main themes: <sup>•</sup> design and consumption; <sup>•</sup> design and te<sup></sup>nology; <sup>•</sup> the design profession; <sup>•</sup> design theory; and <sup>•</sup> design and identities.
Design is de<sup>fi</sup>ned broadly to cover product and furniture design, interior design, fashion design and graphic design. <sup></sup>e new edition is international in scope and emphasises the increasingly global role played by design as it gets nearer to the present day.
Taking a broadly <sup></sup>ronological approa<sup></sup>, Professor Sparke employs historical methods to show how these themes were all implicated by design<sup>’</sup>s progress through the twentieth century and into the twenty-<sup>fi</sup>rst century.
Illustrations throughout the text demonstrate the breadth of design and its multiple contexts, and design examples <sup>–</sup> su<sup></sup> as those of the Sony Walkman, contemporary design in China and James Dyson <sup>–</sup> are used to elaborate key ideas.
Penny Sparke is a Professor of Design History and a Pro Vice-Chancellor (Resear<sup></sup>), Director, Modern Interiors Resear<sup></sup> Centre, Kingston University.
Her resear<sup></sup> interests include modern design and the modern interior with special interest in role of gender.
She is currently resear<sup></sup>ing the meaning of plants and <sup>fl</sup>owers in the modern interior. # An Introduction to Design and Culture 1900 to the Present <sup></sup>ird edition Penny Sparke LONDON AND NEW YORK First published 2013 by Routledge 2 Park Square, Milton Park, Abingdon, Oxon OX14 4RN Simultaneously published in the USA and Canada by Routledge 711 ird Avenue, New York, NY 10017 Routledge is an imprint of the Taylor & Francis Group, an informa business

## Introduction

## Twentieth-century
design and culture revisited Perhaps we should speak of a <sup>‘</sup>political economy of design<sup>’</sup>.<sup>1</sup> <sup></sup>is third edition of this book is a revised and updated version of the 2004 edition, itself an almost completely rewri<sup></sup>en version of the original text I wrote ba<sup></sup> in the early 1980s, entitled An Introduction to Design and Culture in the Twentieth Century.
Si<sup></sup>ing down to rewrite the second edition, I had initially set out with the idea that I would simply add some new sections to accommodate the events of the intervening years.
Very swi<sup></sup>ly, however, I had realized that that was not going to be possible.
Not only had many more things happened in the world of design since the 1980s, my perspective on the subject had also been u<sup></sup>erly transformed by the vast amount of theoretical literature that had emerged since then.
While the broad themes whi<sup></sup> directed the earlier text remained valid, something very substantial had happened in the intervening years, whi<sup></sup> meant that, if the new text was going to constitute a valid contribution from the perspective of the early twenty-<sup>fi</sup>rst century, a mu<sup></sup> more radical overhaul was going to be needed.
Eight years later, yet more signi<sup>fi</sup>cant <sup></sup>anges have occurred and the de<sup>fi</sup>nition that design still had in 2004 is no longer valid. <sup></sup>irteen years into the twenty-<sup>fi</sup>rst century, su<sup></sup> is the impact of the massive global economic, te<sup></sup>nological, social and cultural shi<sup></sup>s that have occurred that design, and designers, can no longer see themselves as continuing either to be rooted in nineteenth-century industrialization or in twentieth-century cultural modernism and mass consumption.
Instead, design is currently looking for a role to play in a world in whi<sup></sup> debt is more of a reality than wealth, in whi<sup></sup> environmental disasters are part of daily life and advanced te<sup></sup>nologies have transformed social relations beyond recognition. <sup></sup>at realignment is still taking place and, to date, it is di<sup>fi</sup>cult to know how it will pan out.
It is the aim of this third edition of An Introduction to Design and Culture to try to capture where design has been, where it is now and to speculate on where it might be going.
Where the 2004 text was concerned, the essential story of design<sup>’</sup>s passage through the twentieth century had, of course, not <sup></sup>anged substantially from the original account.
However, the vast amount of new primary resear<sup></sup> and intellectual debate that had emerged between the mid-1980s and the early twenty-<sup>fi</sup>rst century under the umbrella heading of postmodernism, relating, in particular, to the general area of the culture of consumption, required the concept of design to be approa<sup></sup>ed quite di<sup>f</sup>erently.
Ba<sup></sup> in 1986, I had wri<sup></sup>en that, <sup>‘</sup>Within the framework of industrial capitalism, whi<sup></sup> created it and continues to dominate it in contemporary society, design is <sup></sup>aracterized by a dual alliance with both mass production and mass consumption and these two phenomena have determined nearly all its manifestations.
Like Janus, design looks in two directions at the same time: as a silent quality of all mass-produced goods it plays a generally unacknowledged but vital role in all our lives.
As a named concept within the mass media it is, however, much more visible and generally recognized.<sup>’ </sup>at same statement still held true for the 2004 study. <sup></sup>e di<sup>f</sup>erence, however, was that, while the 1986 text a<sup></sup>nowledged design<sup>’</sup>s important role within consumption, it paid li<sup></sup>le more than lip-service to the ways in whi<sup></sup> designed artefacts and images negotiated the arenas of the social and the cultural through the mediation of consumption. <sup></sup>is was not because it didn<sup>’</sup>t deem it important but rather because, at that time, there was little research available with which to demonstrate the depth of that relationship, the extent to whi<sup></sup>, that is, the modern concept of design was born of market demand and facilitated by mass manufacturing industry, designers and their institutional support structure in the public and private sectors.
Material relating to these last three areas <sup>fi</sup>lled the majority of the pages of the 1986 book but li<sup></sup>le detail was presented with whi<sup></sup> to illuminate the <sup>fi</sup>rst.
As the 2004 book demonstrated in some detail, however, design<sup>’</sup>s relationship with production and the world of professional practice could not, as it is in so many studies emanating from the <sup>fi</sup>eld of <sup>‘</sup>cultural studies<sup>’</sup>, be ignored.
Without it, none of the complex contradictions, that are the very stu<sup>f</sup> of design, can be adequately engaged with.
Having said that, if the rapidly <sup></sup>anging society that <sup></sup>aracterized the era of modernity had not required a visual and material means of expressing its aspirations and its identities, design and, by implication, designers, would not have played su<sup></sup> a prominent role in modern life.
Design and designers are, and have been for many years, a sine qua non of the modern commercial system, ensuring, through the activities of production and consumption, that people<sup>’</sup>s needs and desires (whether consciously a<sup></sup>nowledged or not) are met by the visual and material images and artefacts that enter the marketplace and the spaces we inhabit help us de<sup>fi</sup>ne who we are. <sup></sup>is simple idea provided the starting point for the 2004 introductory study of the relationship between design and culture since 1900. <sup></sup>e structure of the book, whi<sup></sup> has remained in place in this 2013 edition, divides the twentieth and early twenty-<sup>fi</sup>rst centuries into two main periods: 1900<sup>–</sup>1939 and 1940 to the present. <sup></sup>ey correspond, in broad terms, to the historical periods of <sup>‘</sup>modernity<sup>’</sup> and <sup>‘</sup>postmodernity<sup>’</sup> (even though there is not a sudden transition from one to other but, rather, a considerable overlap).
Ea<sup></sup> of the ten <sup></sup>apters is divided into two sections to allow a broad <sup></sup>ronology to guide the narrative. <sup></sup>e <sup>fi</sup>rst sections in the <sup>fi</sup>rst <sup>fi</sup>ve <sup></sup>apters of the book cover the years 1900<sup>–</sup>1914, while their second sections deal with the period 1915<sup>–</sup>1939.
In the second part of the book, the <sup>fi</sup>rst sections of the next <sup>fi</sup>ve <sup></sup>apters cover the period 1940<sup>–</sup>1969, while the second sections of those <sup></sup>apters provide an account of design<sup>’</sup>s trajectory over the period 1970 to the present day.
Because design<sup>’</sup>s role in the marketplace is deemed to be so fundamental to its cultural one, the <sup>fi</sup>rst <sup></sup>apters of ea<sup></sup> of the two main parts of the book focus on the broad context of the <sup>‘</sup>culture of consumption<sup>’</sup>.
Mu<sup></sup> writing related to this subject emerged in the 1980s and <sup>‘</sup>90s and focused on themes as diverse as department stores, shopping, the city, spectacle, gender, ethnicity, class, taste and the in<sup>fl</sup>uence of the mass media, not to mention the vast amount of theoretical work emanating from su<sup></sup> diverse disciplines as history, social history, cultural history, art history, decorative arts history, ar<sup></sup>itectural history, design history, visual culture, material culture, American studies, Italian studies, gender studies, cultural studies, media studies, sociology, anthropology, social psy<sup></sup>ology and cultural geography. <sup></sup>is broad-based body of literature has positioned design, whether overtly or by implication, as a cultural phenomenon.
At the same time, design<sup>’</sup>s inherent multidisciplinarity is su<sup></sup> that it has also been continuously dependent for its <sup></sup>anging self-de<sup>fi</sup>nition over the period in question, on its close links with the worlds of economics, te<sup></sup>nology, art and politics.
However, by the end of the twentieth century, it had become clear that design<sup>’</sup>s main imperative was to create and re<sup>fl</sup>ect meaning in the context of everyday life.
Ba<sup></sup> in the early 1980s that had not been quite so evident.
In the <sup>fi</sup>rst decade of the twenty-<sup>fi</sup>rst century, that primary role has been usurped by the multiple crises that have occurred and design has signi<sup>fi</sup>cantly reoriented itself to become a facilitator for innovation, creativity and <sup></sup>ange across a range of contexts <sup>–</sup> commercial, social and cultural.
As a tool for development <sup>–</sup> a role it has had for some time <sup>–</sup> it has now been adopted by those countries that are entering the global marketplace for the <sup>fi</sup>rst time: China, Taiwan, Singapore, India and Brazil among them.
In my 1986 book, I failed to provide any useful working de<sup>fi</sup>nitions or de<sup>fi</sup>ning frameworks for the two main concepts <sup>–</sup> design and culture <sup>–</sup> that I was at pains to document. <sup></sup>is is still a daunting task, as they are both di<sup>fi</sup>cult, complex concepts whi<sup></sup> have transformed themselves signi<sup>fi</sup>cantly over time and whi<sup></sup> have been de<sup>fi</sup>ned by di<sup>f</sup>erent people at di<sup>f</sup>erent times in di<sup>f</sup>erent ways.
However, there are some useful de<sup>fi</sup>ning <sup></sup>aracteristics that are worth mentioning, if only tentatively.
In linguistic terms, for example, the word <sup>‘</sup>design<sup>’</sup>, with its obvious (although misleading) roots in the Italian disegno and Fren<sup></sup> dessin, can be used both verbally <sup>– ‘</sup>to design and substantively <sup>–</sup> <sup>‘</sup>design<sup>’</sup>, the la<sup></sup>er deriving as a direct result of the former. <sup></sup>is allows one the freedom to treat it ambiguously both as a process and as the result of that process.
From the perspective of this study, therefore, it was deemed important to discuss both <sup>‘</sup>designing<sup>’</sup> and <sup>‘</sup>design <sup>–</sup> and their interface with culture. <sup></sup>is double level of meaning imbues the concept with a particular ri<sup></sup>ness. <sup></sup>e word <sup>‘</sup>culture<sup>’</sup> is even harder to de<sup>fi</sup>ne, denoting, as it can, so many di<sup>f</sup>erent things, from its normative meaning, when it is used to describe highly-valued, highly aestheticized activities <sup>–</sup> su<sup></sup> as opera, poetry, theatre, and <sup>fi</sup>ne art, whi<sup></sup> are believed, by many people, to represent mankind<sup>’</sup>s greatest a<sup></sup>ievements <sup>–</sup> to its anthropological sense in whi<sup></sup> it refers, more simply, to <sup>‘</sup>a way of life<sup>’</sup>.
Interestingly, like <sup>‘</sup>design<sup>’</sup>, the word <sup>‘</sup>culture<sup>’</sup> also has a verbal derivation, linked to the idea of <sup>‘</sup>growing<sup>’</sup> or <sup>‘</sup>nurturing<sup>’</sup>.
In recent times, the word has been transformed into a noun denoting the result of the above activity.
Pu<sup></sup>ing the two words, design and culture, together immediately compounds their complexities and they impact upon ea<sup></sup> other in interesting ways.
Design<sup>’</sup>s relationship with culture, is, for example, signi<sup>fi</sup>cant at both of the la<sup></sup>er<sup>’</sup>s levels, whether <sup>‘</sup>high<sup>’</sup> or <sup>‘</sup>popular<sup>’</sup>.
Indeed, this dualism constitutes an important leitmotif in this text. <sup></sup>e tension between the high-minded idealism underpinning modernist design ideas and the value-free approa<sup></sup> of the postmodernists, who embraced the importance of cultural <sup>‘</sup>di<sup>f</sup>erence<sup>’</sup>, can be seen as one of the dominant themes of the period and the one whi<sup></sup> stimulated the strongest design debates.
In its visual, material and spatial incarnations, design embodied and continues to embody that tension. <sup></sup>e structure of this book re<sup>fl</sup>ects the multiple contexts of design as it evolved through the twentieth century and into the twenty-<sup>fi</sup>rst century and the text emphasizes the ways in whi<sup></sup> it has been both an agent and a mirror of <sup></sup>ange.
While many other accounts of design and material culture see them performing a primarily <sup>‘</sup>illustrative<sup>’</sup> role, this study credits design with having a formative function within society and culture, believing, through its visual and material language, and the ideological values and messages it embodies and carries within it, that it can communicate complex messages.
In turn these can be negotiated and transformed but they are di<sup>fi</sup>cult to ignore.
In this sense, design is seen here as being part of the dynamic process through whi<sup></sup> culture is actually constructed, not merely re<sup>fl</sup>ected.
If the culture of consumption makes design necessary, te<sup></sup>nological progress makes it possible.
Engendered, as a process, by the division of labour whi<sup></sup> played a part in the development of industrial production, design carries te<sup></sup>nology<sup>’</sup>s messages with it into the sociocultural context.
It both mediates the philosophical underpinnings of industrial production <sup>–</sup> its essential rationality for example <sup>–</sup> and the cultural messages of the materials of manufacture, transferring them into the arena of consumption. <sup></sup>e second <sup></sup>apters in the two main parts of this book address design<sup>’</sup>s relationship with the culture of te<sup></sup>nology.
In determining production te<sup></sup>nologies and specifying materials, the designer plays a key part within the production process of goods and images.
In essence, design acts as a bridge between the worlds of production and consumption as the process of <sup>‘</sup>designing<sup>’</sup> is transformed into the sociocultural concept, <sup>‘</sup>design<sup>’</sup>.
Materials are of special signi<sup>fi</sup>cance in this context.
Until they have been <sup>‘</sup>designed and have become material <sup>‘</sup>somethings<sup>’</sup> they have minimal cultural content. <sup></sup>e designer holds enormous power in this respect and can manipulate materials to create multiple meaning, in spite of the fact that they will, inevitably, be transformed, subsequently, within the contexts of consumption and use.
The third chapters in each section of the book turn from the worlds of consumption and production to that of the designer whose primary function is to act a bridge between the two distinct spheres.
It is not the intention here to suggest that he/she (mostly he) is a monolithic, heroic <sup>fi</sup>gure but rather to demonstrate the fact that design is a practice as well as an abstract concept and that the cultural context of that practice is part of the bigger picture.
Design practice has always been fragmented and diverse with enormous <sup></sup>asms separating its specialist <sup>fi</sup>elds <sup>–</sup> the fashion designer and the car designer, for example, have always inhabited di<sup>f</sup>erent universes.
Today, however, those boundaries are being eroded and multidisciplinarity is widely discussed.
It is also a moving target, constantly transforming itself to suit the economic, te<sup></sup>nological, political and cultural climate within whi<sup></sup> it functions at any one time.
Owing to the fact that twentieth century professional design practice emanated from two main roots, there have, however, always been areas of commonality across some specialized design <sup>fi</sup>elds.
While one face of modern design originated within a commercial tradition, manifesting itself either in two-dimensional graphic advertising or in various kinds of display or spectacle whi<sup></sup> overtly set out to promote consumption in various ways, its other important face emerged as an extension of the work of ar<sup></sup>itects who believed they could control the whole visual, material and spatial environment and make the world a be<sup></sup>er place to live in. <sup></sup>ese <sup></sup>apters document the evolving, complex and overlapping routes taken by these two traditions and the design practices they engendered. <sup></sup>ey also tra<sup></sup> the emergence and development of designer culture, one of design<sup>’</sup>s most potent contributions to twentiethcentury life, whi<sup></sup> is predicated on the need, at one level of the market, for design to maintain a link with <sup>fi</sup>ne art in order the ensure it a high level of cultural signi<sup>fi</sup>cance and to distinguish certain products from other, more mundane, goods in the marketplace.
Most signi<sup>fi</sup>cantly, perhaps, this strategic cultural referencing also enabled goods with an overt <sup>‘</sup>design context, frequently made manifest by a marketing or a branding link with a designer<sup>’</sup>s name, to command higher prices than <sup>‘</sup>anonymous<sup>’</sup> products.
While today this still has some relevance for developing countries, it is less visible in countries with vulnerable economies.
In that context, design has largely reverted to its earlier modernist function as an aid to problem solving.
The fourth chapters in each section of the text focus on the ideas and discourses whi<sup></sup> have underpinned design practice since 1900.
As a set of ideas that emerged in the years between 1900 and 1939 as an ideological base-line for design practice, and as a aesthetic ideal whi<sup></sup> in<sup>fl</sup>uenced a new generation of designers, as well as numerous nations whi<sup></sup> sought to distinguish themselves as modern a<sup></sup>er the Second World War, modernism dominates the picture, even though the period of its demise, whi<sup></sup> began in the 1960s, has been almost as long as the one in whi<sup></sup> it was hegemonic.
In the <sup>fi</sup>nal analysis, modernism<sup>’</sup>s impact was felt most acutely by those cultural institutions <sup>–</sup> museums and educational establishments among them whi<sup></sup> embraced its ideology most fully.
However, the concept of modernity, or rather of multiple modernities, experienced by the mass of consumers, had an enormous e<sup>f</sup>ect on everyday life as it was lived by vast numbers of people through the twentieth century.
Designed goods and images played a key role in providing them with access to it. <sup></sup>e last <sup></sup>apters in both sections of the book take as their subject the ways in whi<sup></sup>, once its key contexts and discourses had been established, design has been used by powerful political and economic groupings, nations and corporations in particular, both to de<sup>fi</sup>ne and express their identities and to empower themselves.
In this context, design<sup>’</sup>s ability to embody and express the idea of international modernity was especially meaningful, although local in<sup>fl</sup>ections were inevitably developed to re<sup>fl</sup>ect the special features of the nations and corporations whi<sup></sup> used design as an intrinsic feature of their modernizing strategies. <sup></sup>ese <sup></sup>apters demonstrate the power of design both to form and to express identities, whether for personal, political or commercial ends. <sup></sup>ey also tou<sup></sup> on its capacity to construct and represent the stereotypical <sup></sup>aracteristics of a range of culturallyde<sup>fi</sup>ned categories, su<sup></sup> as class, gender and ethnicity, o<sup>f</sup>ered up for negotiation by individuals in the marketplace. <sup></sup>ese ten <sup></sup>apters o<sup>f</sup>er a picture of design as it relates to culture from a number of key perspectives and describe di<sup>f</sup>erent facets of that complex relationship.
As we have seen <sup>‘</sup>design<sup>’</sup> and <sup>‘</sup>culture<sup>’</sup> are both complex phenomena.
If there is one concept that lies at the heart of their relationship in the twentieth century, however, and whi<sup></sup> holds this study together, however, it is that of <sup>‘</sup>identity<sup>’</sup> or <sup>‘</sup>identities<sup>’</sup>. <sup></sup>e way in whi<sup></sup> individuals and groups depend upon the mass media as a means of de<sup>fi</sup>ning themselves is a key <sup></sup>aracteristic of modern life.
Increasingly, through the twentieth century, values were communicated to people less and less through interaction with their local communities and more and more through the mass media. <sup></sup>rough its inherent relationship with industrial production, a key agent of mass mediation and its presence in the mass-disseminated goods, images, spaces and services that resulted from that process, design became a key component of all the messages that were communicated.
To extend Marshall McLuhan<sup>’</sup>s analysis of the mass media, not only is the <sup>‘</sup>medium<sup>’</sup> the <sup>‘</sup>message<sup>’</sup>, it is the design of the medium whi<sup></sup> is crucial in this context, as it determines the way in whi<sup></sup> the message is read and negotiated.
Arguably, by the late twentieth century, design had become more signi<sup>fi</sup>cant than the medium itself, as it played an increasingly ubiquitous role in in<sup>fl</sup>uencing the ways in whi<sup></sup> individuals and groups de<sup>fi</sup>ned themselves.
In other words the lifestyle implications of the media became the most signi<sup>fi</sup>cant part of their messages.
Design expresses itself visually, materially, spatially and virtually, primarily (although not exclusively) in the context of consumption and, in that context, it is frequently negotiated through the agency of taste whi<sup></sup> underpins consumer <sup></sup>oice.
It is a complex language, however, whi<sup></sup> can operate, simultaneously, as representation and as material actuality. <sup></sup>is way of thinking about design prioritizes its role within identity formation.
In the words of the cultural critic, Grant McCra<sup></sup>en, <sup>‘</sup>without consumer goods, certain acts of self-de<sup>fi</sup>nition and collective de<sup>fi</sup>nition in this culture would be impossible<sup>’</sup>.<sup>2</sup> <sup></sup>ese de<sup>fi</sup>nitions are, inevitably, as numerous as the individuals and the groups that relate to them.
At the same time, however, a number of key overar<sup></sup>ing identities emerged in the twentieth century, notable among them one we call <sup>‘</sup>modern<sup>’</sup>. <sup></sup>e visual style developed by the modernists <sup>–</sup> simple, undecorated, etc. <sup>–</sup> was one way of recognizing the modern but the de<sup>fi</sup>nition of modernity went beyond that to include the experience of engaging in a lifestyle whi<sup></sup> embraced all that modern life had to o<sup>f</sup>er, from te<sup></sup>nologically sophisticated goods to access, for women, to the public sphere.
Inevitably, that experience varied according the situations of individuals or groups whi<sup></sup> were dependent upon the vagaries of class, gender, race, etc.
Design became everyone<sup>’</sup>s bridge to their own brand of modernity.
In the <sup>fi</sup>rst half of the twentieth century, the term <sup>‘</sup>design<sup>’</sup> can be understood as being synonymous with modern design, although its <sup></sup>aracteristics varied according to whose modernity was in question and, in certain instances, modern could even be expressed by the adoption of an historical style. <sup></sup>e la<sup></sup>er half of the century presents a more complex picture, one whi<sup></sup>, in this book, is <sup></sup>aracterized by the umbrella term <sup>‘</sup>postmodernism<sup>’</sup>, used as a cat<sup></sup>-all to describe the pluralistic cultures that emerged in those years.
Within postmodernity, arguably, design had an even more integrated role, its very existence being one of the prerequisites of the <sup>‘</sup>condition<sup>’</sup>.
However, even within postmodernity, the concepts of <sup>‘</sup>design<sup>’</sup> and <sup>‘</sup>modernity<sup>’</sup> continued to be linked and it remained very hard to disengage them from ea<sup></sup> other. <sup></sup>is text is dependent upon a number of theoretical texts and situates itself within the body of literature relating to the culture of consumption whi<sup></sup> emerged in the 1980s and 1990s.
Feminist ideas developed in the same decade have also been a strong in<sup>fl</sup>uence.
It is especially indebted to two texts published in 1986: Andreas Huyssen<sup>’</sup>s After the Great Divide: Modernism, Mass Culture and Postmodernism and Pierre Bourdieu<sup>’</sup>s Distinction: A Social Critique of the Judgement of Taste. <sup></sup>e ideas articulated in the <sup>fi</sup>rst book can be seen to relate directly to design, even though Huyssen stopped short of pushing very hard in that direction himself, as it was not the focus of his study.
Bourdieu<sup>’</sup>s book, wri<sup></sup>en <sup>fi</sup>rst in the 1970s, but translated into English in the mid-1980s, also provided an important framework for this study of design and culture.<sup>3</sup> It positioned design within a sociocultural framework whi<sup></sup> made sense of it and whi<sup></sup> took <sup></sup>orstein Veblen<sup>’</sup>s innovative ideas of the late nineteenth century forward several stages.
Although mu<sup></sup> of the material in Bourdieu<sup>’</sup>s book is dated now, and the case studies were very speci<sup>fi</sup>cally Fren<sup></sup>, his proposition that the exercising of taste underpins both the shape and the dynamic of modern society and culture is one whi<sup></sup> has still not been fully a<sup></sup>nowledged, so preoccupied, until recently, have historians and theorists been with post-Marxist ideas and with prioritizing production over consumption.
Bourdieu<sup>’</sup>s ideas help <sup></sup>allenge the modernist framework whi<sup></sup> has tended to dominate most accounts of design to date.
In trying to encompass a wider spectrum of design activities and objects, the 2004 text was more ambitious than its 1986 predecessor. <sup></sup>e primary focus on product design remained but more e<sup>f</sup>ort was made to include fashion, graphic, interior, environmental and, in this 2013 edition, environmental, interaction, social, inclusive and virtual design, where appropriate. <sup></sup>is was not simply for inclusiveness for its own sake but rather because the author has come to understand that modes of professional practice and ideas <sup>fl</sup>owed from one area to another through the twentieth and early twenty-<sup>fi</sup>rst centuries and that consumers and users do not compartmentalize designed goods and images in the same way as the manufacturing industry.
Ideas generated within specialized design <sup>fi</sup>elds went on to in<sup>fl</sup>uence others over a period of time. <sup></sup>e role played by identity within fashion design, for example, permeated the world of interior decoration in the early century and subsequently went on to play a role within car design.
Similarly, the emphasis on spectacle, visible in the early twentieth century shop-window display, moved into objects themselves through the agency of industrial designers.
Also, the aesthetic relationship that consumers have long had with decorative art objects <sup>–</sup> ceramics, glass and textiles among them <sup>–</sup> has <sup>fi</sup>nally been transferred to computergenerated images. <sup></sup>ese are just some of the insights that it has been possible to gain by considering design across a range of media and within a historical context.
Most importantly, however, this text sets out to avoid the historicizing tendencies of the 1986 edition.
In spite of e<sup>f</sup>orts to withstand their o<sup></sup>en strident claims, the dominance of modernist texts in the early 1980s was su<sup></sup> that the 1986 study was inevitably in<sup>fl</sup>uenced by them and unwi<sup></sup>ingly reproduced their rhetoric and their tendency to reductivism to some extent.
All that has <sup></sup>anged since the disciplines of cultural and media studies have matured and the impact of postmodern ideas in the broadest sense has become greater.
It is now mu<sup></sup> easier to understand that design had, and continues to have, no <sup>fi</sup>xed de<sup>fi</sup>nition or meanings nor one ideal path to follow.
Rather, it is a constantly transforming concept, re<sup>fl</sup>ected in a set of practices and in<sup>fl</sup>uenced by a broad context of <sup></sup>anging ideologies and discourses that have a<sup>f</sup>ected its shi<sup></sup>ing parameters.
If a discourse of design can be developed it must be one whi<sup></sup> recognizes the high level of relativism, pragmatism and contextualization that has determined the concept<sup>’</sup>s past and whi<sup></sup> will, undoubtedly, continue to a<sup>f</sup>ect its future.
Design will continue to be in<sup>fl</sup>uenced by consumption, the fashion system, identities of all kinds, production, whether industrial or cra<sup></sup>-based, as well as by broader ideologies and discourses and economic, environmental and social conditions and crises outside its control.
It is a constantly moving target, although its future is, like everything else, signi<sup>fi</sup>cantly in<sup>fl</sup>uenced by its past.
In short, the picture of design and designers is constantly being repainted. <sup></sup>is book can only, therefore, present a sket<sup></sup> of it, as the colours will constantly <sup></sup>ange.
Part 1 Design and modernity, 1900<sup>–</sup>1939

## 1 Consuming
modernity

## Conspicuous
consumption and the expansion of taste e department stores had fostered an extravagant taste not only for clothes but also for ‘things one might do without’.
Although the term ‘design’ did not have any common currency until the middle years of the twentieth century, the idea of imbuing goods and images with aesthetic and functional aracteristics that aracted consumers and met the needs of users has a long history. at history was closely linked to the development of modern society and culture.
As a modern concept, design developed as a direct result of the expansion of the market for consumer goods and the democratization of taste.
For centuries, hand-made furnishings, ceramics, glass, metalwork, dress, printed artefacts and carriages had impacted on the lives of members of the upper classes, acting as providers of comfort, as markers of propriety, as the glue of social, family and gender relations and as visible signs of fashionableness, taste and social status.
As modernity influenced the lives of ever larger numbers of people, however, design – the visual and conceptual component of the mass-produced goods and images that facilitated mass production, made goods aractive to a mass market and helped to give meaning to people’s everyday lives – took on the role that, for the social élite, had been performed up until that point by the decorative arts.
In both Europe and the USA, industrialization began to create new levels of social mobility and the increased access to goods began to blur traditional class distinctions.
Indeed, as increasing numbers of consumers embraced new goods, new classes emerged.
Within that context, the link between design and taste was reinforced.
Gradually, as industrially produced goods became more accessible, designed goods and images took over from the decorative arts the task of demarcating social diference and becoming the key messengers of fashionableness and modernity.
There has been much debate about the moment at which the process of modernization first came to be formed by and expressed through material culture.
Some studies have documented conspicuous consumption as a sixteenth-century phenomenon, while others have located it in the eighteenth century. e historian, Lorna Weatherill, for example, has demonstrated the way in whi, in the laer century, the levels of the ownership of new goods in Britain varied considerably in urban and rural areas. ‘Saucepans’, she explained, ‘associated with cookery on enclosed stoves, were four times as common in London and in other towns as they were in the countryside, where about only one in twenty households used them.
Earthenware, although used in farming as well as in households, was more common in London than in provincial towns’.
Weatherill’s groundbreaking study claimed that mass demand preceded and, indeed, helped to bring about the anges in manufacturing teniques associated with the industrial revolution.
She provided data relating to both aristocratic and demotic consumption and to the ways in whi certain goods – textiles, ceramics and metal goods in particular – played key social and cultural roles for new consumers.
In the nineteenth century, the middle classes expanded in number and their capacity to consume continued to grow.
Studies of the paerns of their consumption in Britain are few and far between, however, and the picture that is presented by those historians who have addressed that question has tended to focus on goods destined for the domestic arena.
Work has been undertaken, for example, on the customers of high-end manufacturers, su as the furniture maker and interior decorating firm, Holland and Sons, but there is still mu more to be done on the consumption paerns of people inhabiting the other end of the social spectrum.
W.
Hamish Fraser has provided an overview of the way in whi the expansion of the nineteenthcentury British market for manufactured goods was a direct result of the growth in the population, an increase in spending power and of shis in taste that encouraged people to transfer their spending from one set of goods to another.
In the 1860s, he explained, more gas cookers became available as a result of a fall in the price of gas; by 1914, new household objects, su as the US Bissell carpet sweeper, came on to the British market; following its success in the US marketplace, the domestic vacuum cleaner made its appearance in Britain at the turn of the century and, by the first decade of the new century, the bicycle and the automobile had become common appendages of the urban scene. e strategies involved in catering for, sustaining and expanding the new mass market subsumed the question of design through its links with advertising, marketing and retailing.
6 While visual elaboration enhanced the appeal of the new goods, tenological novelty played a key role in most consumer oices at that time.
Given that many consumers saw it as a threat, however, visual strategies were sometimes employed to conceal that novelty. at was especially the case in some of the new domestic electrical products to whi surface paern was oen added to enhance familiarity and relieve anxiety (see Figure 2.3).
While Britain was the first country to witness the advent of mass consumption, it also emerged in the USA and in a number of European countries.
Although it took longer than in Britain to bring about an expansion of production figures, when it finally did so in the USA a mu more dramatic and rapid growth of consumer activity and a more forwardlooking approa towards the development of new, tenological products destined for both the public and the private spheres could be observed.
In his study of the anging nature of the American middle classes, the historian, Riard L.
Bushman, has shown how, as a direct result of material and environmental developments, they had aieved refinement by the midnineteenth century.
Gentility, he argued, went hand in hand with the emergence of a sophisticated model of domesticity, the growth of cities and the acquisition of taste.
Focusing more specifically upon anging consumption paerns and their relationship with material culture, the essays in S.
J.
Bronner’s collection, Consuming Visions: Accumulation and Display of Goods in America 1880– <sub>1920</sub>, have documented the impact of modernity on American society as expressed through consumers’ oices of their material environments. e growth of consumption was understood as a key feature of the emerging modern world.
Karen Hultenen’s essay has described the subtle transformation of the parlour into the living room and the modernizing efect of the female inhabitant’s enhanced association with that newly defined space.
Consumption had a material face that was visible in both the public and private spheres.
Although historians have tended to move away from the idea that those spheres were entirely separate – men inhabiting the laer, that is, and women being confined to the former – the anging material culture of the two diferent spheres was largely specific to ea in the years leading up to 1914.
Cultural historians have discussed the public sphere at length, in particular the expansion of city culture in the late nineteenth and early twentieth century, focusing on the impact of modernity on people’s anging experience of everyday life. ey have said less about the concept of design, however, in spite of the fact that the visual, material and spatial environment undoubtedly played a key role within those transformations. e extensive work on the concept of the <sub>flaneur</sub> – the poet, Charles Baudelaire’s, term for the male city wanderer – and Walter Benjamin’s writings about the commercial face of the city, has explored the new experience of the city and equated it with modernity.
Elizabeth Wilson has concentrated on women’s experience of the city in that period, seeing it as an escape from domesticity.
While the modern urban experience has been described in terms of the high level of spectacle visible to city dwellers, most accounts have privileged its reception rather than its production or its designed components.
Although the idea of modernity existed before aritects and designers had created a visual, material and spatial representation of it, the first signs of a man-made shi in the look of the city itself – whether London, Paris, Vienna or New York – emerged at the end of the nineteenth century. e impact of, first, gas and subsequently of electric lighting transformed the city into a dramatically new kind of night-time environment, while the advent of new retailing outlets, department stores in particular, transformed the act of shopping and, perhaps more significantly, that of window shopping.
New objects of transportation, trains and cars among them, also contributed to the new experience of everyday urban life.
The American cultural historian, William Leach, has written at length about the way in whi the new plate-glass store windows created a level of spectacle for the city dweller or visitor.
Seen from the street, the illuminated contents of those public theatres ofered a new and dramatic form of popular entertainment that was created by a new visualizer, the show-window display artist.
His task was to create a dramatic visual focus for the <sub>flaneurs</sub>, whose growing presence transformed the streets of the city. e overtly commercial role of window displays ofered a foretaste of what later came to be called design for industry and the nineteenth-century store display artists were the forerunners of the inter-war creative artists, known as industrial designers, who went on to transform the appearance of goods themselves rather than merely that of the frame that surrounded them. <sub>Figure 1.1</sub> Diens and Jones department store in early twentieth-century London (courtesy of London Metropolitan Arives) Literary historians, social and economic historians and aritectural historians have provided numerous accounts of the birth of the department store (Figure 1.1). e contemporary fascination with that subject on the part of historians emanated from a desire to capture the experience of modernity and to uncover the roots of contemporary consumer and commodity culture.
Feminist historians have been particularly active in the field, seeing the department store as middle-class women’s first encounter with the public sphere and commercial culture. e department stores established in the second half of the nineteenth century consolidated the modern emphasis on the experience of seeing and, as a consequence, the marginalization of the other senses.
In sharp contrast to the act of buying goods from a market stall, which had involved touch and smell as much as sight, the dominance of the eye aracterized the modern way of shopping and of interacting with the modern world. at shi marked a moment when the material culture of modernity took on an extra level of potency and became a key aracteristic of the modern world.
Within the public sphere, numerous visual, material and environmental markers of modernity – from the new objects of public and private transport, to dress, to posters to spaces within public buildings – were in evidence on the late nineteenth century.
So novel were the engineered forms of many of the new objects of transportation – bicycles, railway trains, transatlantic liners and early aeroplanes among them – that they took on an iconic significance.
Indeed, so powerful was their visual and symbolic impact that, a lile later, modernist aritects and designers were to take their aesthetic lead from them. e rational kiten of the 1920s, for example, took its inspiration from Pullman trains, where space was at a premium.
In the years leading up to 1914, designers began to borrow from one new form to create another. e smooth, aerodynamic forms of early automobiles, for example, were inspired by the bulbous shape of the prows of boats and the fuselages of aeroplanes. e new aesthetic that was thus created owed its existence to engineers but, re-interpreted by designers, it was transformed into a visual marker of modernity.
While the material culture of the public sphere openly embraced modernity, the private sphere was slower to respond.
Nowhere was that more apparent than in the domestic interior, especially in those areas of the home where everyday life and its accompanying rituals were enacted (Figure 1.2).
In turn-of-the-century USA, for example, following the advice of Edith Wharton and Ogden Codman that was articulated in their book on the subject, the interior fashion of the day favoured the Fren eighteenthcentury style, understood by a newly ri sector of society as the perfect messenger of good taste.
Even in that most conservative of contexts, however, modernization was present in the form of new tenologies, su as gas and electric lighting and heating.
While not every aspect of material culture moved equally quily towards modernity, there was a strong sense, at the turn of the century, that important transformations were taking place that were anging the experience of everyday life for increasing numbers of people. <sub>Figure 1.2</sub> Late nineteenth-century middle-class American parlour (courtesy of the Society for the Preservation of New England Antiquities) Dress, or fashion as it had become for the majority of people by that time, played a role in both the private and the public spheres.
Several studies have looked in detail at the relationship of men and women’s clothing with modernity.
Elizabeth Wilson, for example, has shown how fashion and modernity developed hand in hand with ea other in the urban context, especially in the formation of identities, while Christopher Breward’s account of male clothing in the period has explained that men were not, as has frequently been claimed, operating outside the fashion cycle or the world of consumption. e widespread idea that men’s dress was standardized at that time was, in fact, formed later, influenced by the modernist belief that men were governed by rationality rather than desire and that they resisted the urge to join the fashion cycle.
As the Fren cultural critic, Roland Barthes, has explained, the language of material culture in the context of consumption operates simultaneously on two levels and is dependent upon an important contradiction.
To follow the logic of the capitalist economy, he has claimed, it has to evoke desire while also aligning itself to the rationality of the production system.
From the outset, design was aracterized by that dual alliance, its main <sub>raison</sub> <sub>d’être</sub> being its role within industrial production but its primary function being to stimulate desire in the marketplace.
It was the task of the designer to reconcile those conflicting requirements.
In 1899, the social scientist, orstein Veblen, published his seminal text, <sub>Theory</sub> <sub>of</sub> <sub>the</sub> <sub>Leisure</sub> <sub>Class</sub>, an early aempt to analyze the system underpinning conspicuous consumption.
Later, Georg Simmel also set out to unravel the same conundrum.
Veblen focused his study on the way in whi society was driven by conspicuous leisure.
In the consumption of women’s fashionable dress, he recognized the existence of a social process that applied to that of other goods as well, although with less intensity. e situation, he observed, was aracterized by fashionably dressed women acting as markers of their husband’s social position and continually seeking new, ever more fashionable clothes in emulation of their immediate social superiors (Figure 1.3).
He isolated the concept of upward emulation: the phenomenon, that is, whi, according to Veblen, drove the continual process of conspicuous consumption and fashion ange.
Although he did not focus his aention on design, on the process that made fashionable dress aractive to consumers in the first place, it was a necessary (albeit unanowledged) component, nonetheless, of the social process he had described. <sub>Figure</sub> <sub>1.3</sub> Late nineteenth-century women’s dress from <sub>Myra’s</sub> <sub>Journal</sub> <sub>of</sub> <sub>Dress</sub> <sub>and</sub> <sub>Fashion</sub>, 1 September 1885, published by Weldon, London (courtesy the V&A images at the Victoria and Albert Museum, London) Veblen was more interested in the sociocultural function of dress than in its inherent aracteristics.
For the designers who created fashion items, however – among them the Fren couturiers, Charles Frederi Worth, Jacques Doucet and Jeanne Paquin – detail was everything. eir task was to continually refine the language of dress through the application of new colours, decorative details, shapes and levels of elaboration, su that they were continually desirable.
Although in outlining his theory of upward emulation Veblen referred to dress, it was equally relevant to a range of other visual and material artefacts, among them interiors, furnishings, toasters, knives and forks and eventually automobiles.
So fundamental was the role of design in maintaining social stability and cohesion in the context of continuous ange that the existence of visually skilled individuals who could conceptualize and continually renew the visual appearance of consumer goods was paramount. e goods in question continued to include the traditional decorative arts, whi incorporated the safety of familiarity and were therefore risk-free for those consumers who were insecure about their taste.
As the idea of fashion and modernity began to afect the lives of increasing numbers of people, however, the two-dimensional world of commercial promotions that embraced advertisements, magazines and the paages created for branded goods, began to reflect that fact, making consumers aware of what was available to them and communicating to them ideals to whi they could aspire. e growth of modern advertising and of modern mass-media magazines, aimed in the first instance at women, served to create new levels of desire and to accelerate the growth of modern consumer culture.
Women’s magazines, especially those focusing on fashion and the elaboration of the home, played a crucial role in the expanding consumer culture of the second half of the nineteenth century.
Margaret Beetham has provided an account of the expansion of those magazines in Britain in the years in question, while Jennifer Scanlon has documented the development of the American woman’s magazine, <sub>The Ladies’ Home Journal</sub>.
Both studies have emphasized the ways in whi those publications served to position women at the heart of modern consumer culture.
As well as acting as consumer guides, however, they also contained advertisements for the countless consumer goods on ofer in the marketplace.
In tandem with the growth of magazines, the numbers of household advice books also swelled in at that time on both sides of the Atlantic, ofering yet another source of information for housewives keen to perform their tasks properly, as well as a wide range of idealized images of domesticity to inspire them.
One of the subtlest ways in whi advertising and marketing penetrated the consciousness of consumers was through the use of brands.
Changes in retailing systems from market stalls to fixed shops and urban department stores put a greater emphasis on the roles of paaging and branding (Figure 1.4).
Rather than buying loose foodstufs, shoppers increasingly bought prepaed goods that had been prepared in factories and distributed in bulk to retail outlets.
Susan Strasser has described the anges that took place in retailing in those years and the accompanying rise of branding as a means of identifying goods.
It was the era in whi the names of companies, used as brands – Kellogg’s and Hoover among them – came to replace those of the commodities themselves in the minds of consumers.
Paaging and branding necessitated the emergence of yet another group of visualizers, whose role was to create aractive, memorable images that helped promote the brand over the product.
Although brand names were oen visible on paets, they were essentially immaterial concepts that sold products through their appeal to a desirable lifestyle. <sub>Figure</sub> <sub>1.4</sub> Late nineteenth-century paaging in the USA (courtesy of the Arives Center, National Museum of American History, Smithsonian Institution) e rise of branded goods in the USA represented a major shi in the way in whi goods were bought and sold and created a new link between design and marketing that was to become increasingly complex over the next century.
Companies found names for their products that evoked particular lifestyles. e Pisburgh Reduction Company, for example, a manufacturer of aluminium kitenware items, coined the name <sub>Wearever</sub> for its range of products, building on the assumption that their potential consumers were rational beings who wanted value for money.
It employed artists to develop a range of magazine advertisements to promote the <sub>Wearever</sub> range and shops were encouraged to place banners in their shop windows with the name emblazoned on it.
A sophisticated visual promotional framework was thus developed around the campaign to sell aluminium pots and pans.
Importantly, it required the input of visually skilled specialists in the areas of graphics, paaging and show-window display.
In the years before 1914, however, few aempts were made to make goods themselves – aluminium saucepans, for example – look aractive.
Rather, they retained a utilitarian appearance and their desirability was communicated through the visual information that accompanied them. at early means of conveying the added value of mass-produced items represented a first stage in a process that eventually led to the emergence, in the inter-war years, of industrial design.
By that time, manufacturers had realized that the visual impact of goods themselves gave them a competitive edge that advertisements, paaging and shop-window displays could not aieve on their own.
By the early years of the twentieth century, in its aempt to stimulate and satisfy consumer desire, the commercial world had developed a set of sophisticated marketing strategies that involved a wide range of visualizing and conceptualizing practices. e expanded market, the phenomenon of new consumers using visual, material and spatial culture as a means of defining and communicating their identities and social aspirations and the availability of more and more goods, images and services in the marketplace, combined to create the necessary bacloth for the emergence of a concept of modern design that began to merge those strands.
Most significantly, design, as it came to be called, was aracterized by its unique position at the interface between consumption and production and by its ability to relate both to the irrational behaviour of consumers and the increasingly rational process of mass manufacturing.
Above all, its ability to represent modernity made it one of the key social and cultural forces of the early twentieth century.
By 1914, the framework was in place for a new, modern visual, material and spatial culture that was defined by its role within conspicuous consumption and the requirements of the marketplace.

## Consumer
culture and modernity Consumer culture is in important respects the culture of the modern west.<sup>28</sup> In the years between the two world wars, more and more inhabitants of the western industrialized world began to engage with modernity in a multitude of ways.
Sometimes, as the social historian, Sally Alexander, has so evocatively shown, it was expressed by gestures as simple as a tou of lipsti or the puf of a cigaree.
At other moments it was represented by the wholesale reshaping of the visual, material and spatial environment, as demonstrated, for example, by New York’s 1939 World’s Fair.
Modernity existed to the extent to whi people could imagine the future and bring that vision to bear on the present.
It was frequently experienced as an idealized concept, something that promised to add value to people’s lives and that went beyond need into the world of desire.
Presented to them through the mediation of mass manufactured goods, magazines, films and advertisements, it had something of the unaainable about it while being, at the same time, aainable.
Luxurious, it was also democratic.
Modernity’s key aracteristic, therefore, as it was presented to the inhabitants of the industrialized world, was its inbuilt ambivalence.
Designers, paid to be imaginative and visually innovative, played a key part envisioning modernity, in ensuring that it remained just out of rea and in appearing to resolve its inherent contradictions, if only temporarily. e outward signs of modernity continued to be most obviously visible in the public arena.
Europe and the USA witnessed the arrival of urbanization and increasing levels of wealth in those years.
As a result, consumption grew dramatically, with far-reaing efects on the mass environment.
Car ownership grew, for example, creating in its wake a roadside material culture of motels, drive-in cinemas and petrol stations (Figure 1.5).
Also in the public arena the consumption of goods for personal embellishment – from fros to make-up – and the enhanced visibility of women meant that city life was visually transformed. e ever increasing impact of graphic advertising and commercial display added to that efect, providing a mass environment that, in urban and suburban centres, was visually transformed.
Those changes were driven by the commercial machine that underpinned the expansion of consumer culture.
It was especially successful in the USA, where the art of marketing was at its most sophisticated.
Mu has been wrien about its development.
Susan Strasser and R.
S.
Tedlow, for example, have documented the rise of mass marketing in the USA.
Case studies have demonstrated how marketing ensured that mass-produced goods reaed a large audience of consumers.
In her essay, <sub>The</sub> <sub>Marketing</sub> <sub>of</sub> <sub>Cutex</sub> <sub>in</sub> <sub>America, 1916–1935</sub>, for example, Kate Forde has shown how the J.
Walter ompson Advertising Company set about selling nail varnish to a female mass market.<sup>32</sup> e Cutex brand was heavily advertised through women’s magazines, the readership of whi was expanding exponentially.
Forde has explained that JWT’s strategy was to sell the name of Cutex, in line with a range of other modern hygiene products, among them Borax, Kleenex, Lux and Pyrex, as if it were a kind of deity. e use of strikingly modernist paaging was also an important selling point.
Kathy Peiss’s essay, <sub>Hope</sub> <sub>in</sub> <sub>a</sub> Jar: The Making of America’s Beauty Culture<sup>,</sup> <sup>has</sup> <sup>also</sup> <sup>explained</sup> <sup>that</sup> consumer fantasies were created, controlled and fulfilled by the commercial system, whi embraced design .<sup>35</sup> <sub>Figure 1.5</sub> Texaco gas station designed by Teague Associates, USA, 1930s (© Walter Dorwin Teague Associates) e financial hardships of the 1930s did not dampen people’s desire to consume a particular range of goods – cars, refrigerators and other domestic appliances among them. e new products of the tenological industries came with a vision of modernity built into them.
Cars, for example, were manufactured by new production teniques and, from the late 1920s, styled to appeal to a spectrum of consumers.
General Motors’ (GM) range included its up-market Cadillac and its down-market Bui at that time. e market segmentation of goods not only reflected consumer diference but it also played a key role in creating it. e result was the emergence of a subtle consumer–design relationship that increasingly applied right across the social spectrum.
While it was not possible for GM to predict consumer tastes, as Sally Clarke has explained, its venture into industrial design constituted a business risk that it was, nevertheless, willing to take. e democratization of taste and luxury brought about by design’s alliance with mass production industry was intensified through the interwar years across the industrialized world, bringing with it a new mass sensitivity to stylistic ange. e fashion cycle, described by orstein Veblen some decades earlier, became a widespread reality, particularly in those areas of material culture – fashionable dress and motor-cars in particular – that were most susceptible to it.
Along the way, a gendering of material culture was established whereby women used their dress, their beauty products and their interiors to express their fashion awareness, and thence their social standing (Figure 1.6), whereas men began to invest their status and identities in the new consumer maines. <sub>Figure 1.6</sub> A modern woman, 1930s (courtesy of the author) Mu was wrien about the female consumer of the inter-war years.
Within the period, it was widely anowledged that women were the key consumers.
In <sub>Selling Mrs.
Consumer</sub> of 1929, the household management expert turned consumer advisor, Christine Frederi, described what she believed to be the aracteristics of ‘feminine consumption’.
In a section entitled ‘Guessing at Mrs.
Consumer’s Character’ she explained that, ‘Mrs.
Consumer habitually proceeds more along the lines of instinct than upon theory or reason and accommodates herself more readily to practical realities.
Man is more thoroughly theoretical’.
In his article, ‘Consuming Brotherhood: Men’s Culture, Style and Recreation as Consumer Culture, 1880–1930’, Mark A.
Swiencii has claimed, however, that, ‘men were a very large and important consuming constituency’.
His evidence indicates that men were indeed significant consumers in the period, with their range of possible consumables including ready-to-wear clothing, sports equipment and recreational activities.
Unlike women, Swiencii has argued, men did not acquire their goods from shops and department stores but rather from a range of nonretail outlets, su as saloons, lodges, clubs, barbershops and dime theatres.
Designed goods, images and services clearly played an important role in both motivating sales and meeting the needs and desires of both men and women, albeit in diferent ways.
In his book, <sub>Auto Opium</sub>, the sociologist David Gartman has argued that the growing emphasis upon colour and appearance in consumer decision making was a result of the fact that women were increasingly involved in purasing decisions.
Although, he has suggested, men were also involved, they did not admit that they were equally interested in the visual aspects of everyday life.
He has also explained that, with the advent of mass manufacturing, inasmu as the assemblers of mass-produced cars also became their consumers, the worlds of production and consumption became increasingly intertwined.
A form of compensation for the loss incurred by their labour being sold to the capitalist system was thus provided.
Automobile graphic advertising appealed to a world on wheels and was designed accordingly. e design historian, David Jeremiah, has researed the expansion of petrol stations in Britain in the period, showing how they, ‘irreversibly anged the shape of the rural and urban landscape’. at ange did not come about without resistance, however, as many people saw it as a regreable influence from America that was ruining the environment.
From the outset, consumer culture had its aggressive opponents who did not want the natural landscape polluted by unnatural constructions and garish graphics.
Early twentieth-century aluminium cooking pots, for example, were believed to spread disease and the new emical substances contained in nail polish, su as the nitrocellulose, were also thought to be dangerous.
Whether in the form of graphic advertisements, paaging or as a component of goods themselves, design played an important role in ofseing su anxieties and appeasing consumers through the evocation of a pleasant, beautiful world in whi progress was always depicted as benevolent.
Arguably, the advent of the motor-car was the most radical modernizing material cultural force of the interwar years, most dramatically in the USA but also significantly in Europe.
In their preface to <sub>The Automobile and</sub> <sub>American</sub> <sub>Culture</sub>, the editors, David L.
Lewis and Laurence Goldstein, have discussed society’s ambivalent relationship with the automobile. ‘We still complain’ they wrote, ‘that vehicles disturb tranquillity, pollute the environment, kill us of in large numbers, and strain our poetbooks.
But only a handful of us have traded motor vehicles for mopeds, mu less bicycles’.
In his study of the influence of the motor-car on British society in the period in question, Sean O’Connell has emphasized the inherent masculinity of that new form of transportation whi, he has argued, ‘has ultimately prevented millions of women from taking to the driving seat’.<sup>45</sup> Designed public environments, especially those linked to work, shared leisure and shopping, also played a key role within the formation of urban modernity.
In all the modern metropolises of the western industrialized world, shops and the experience of shopping were important agents of modernization for large sections of society.
Building on pre-1914 innovations when the means of selling to a mass market were first put in place, shops presented themselves as overtly modern. e efect was a circular one, in that the new environments simultaneously reflected and created a modern consumer culture.
Indeed, shops were among the first buildings the interior spaces of whi were influenced by the modern styles of the day, from the austere form of high modernism in the most up-market stores (London’s Simpsons of Piccadilly, for example) to the more decorative, Americaninfluenced curved forms of streamlining, sometimes referred to as ‘modernistic’ and the Fren-influenced ‘<sub>moderne</sub>’ style.
Fren designers played a special role in the modernization of the shopping environment.
In that country, manufacturing was dominated by the luxury trades – those of haute couture, perfume, interior decoration and cra-made furniture among them.
Shopping was geared, therefore, to the socially and financially privileged, who purased their goods from élite outlets, while it also ofered aspiring consumers access to a luxurious lifestyle. e laer’s first experience of modernity was oen in a department store or a boutique.
In her study of 1920s Paris, Tag Gronberg has explained that the ‘<sub>devanture</sub> <sub>de</sub> <sub>boutique</sub>’ (shop front) was ‘frequently cited as the most appropriate means of modernizing Paris’. e model of modernity she presented was strongly bound up with the concepts of fashion and luxury.
Modernity was especially evident in post-war dress.
Aer the First World War, a revolution in women’s dress and general profile meant that the so flowing forms of the early century were replaced by a more linear, angular silhouee. e idealized female body was dramatically transformed as fashionable women aspired to a look that owed mu the modernist ‘maine aesthetic’.
Men’s idealized bodies also anged to reflect a new image of modernity.
Transformations in hairstyling were particularly apparent.
New tenology allowed women to curl their new short hair and present it in the fashionable styles of the day.
Innovative hairdressers, su as Antoine of Paris, turned hairdressing into an art form that was emulated in urban centres across the globe.
The spaces in which such activities took place combined the advances made possible by tenology in the public sphere and the traditionally private and intimate activity of having one’s hair dressed at home, resulting in a new democratized fusion of luxury and modernity.<sup>47</sup> Even within the tradition-oriented arena of the home, modernity was also making its mark.
It was especially noticeable in kitens and bathrooms where there was lile resistance to the improvements that the new technologies could make.
The kitchen was affected by advances in organization, inspired by the scientific management advice provided by Christine Frederi in the early century.
Continuous work surfaces were installed by those householders who could aford them and the new laboursaving appliances – as mu status objects as time-savers – were consumed in large numbers by the ’30s. e refrigerator, in particular, took on an iconic significance at that time, representing as it did the arrival of tenological modernity in kitens across the globe. e entry of modernity into the domestic arena went hand in hand with the housewife’s anging role and status.
From a rational scientist, in arge of her laboratory, she was transformed into a caring wife, mother and consumer.
By the 1930s, however, she had turned into a arming hostess, inviting visitors into her home and showing them her impressive modern goods.
A seductive idealized image was created by manufacturers and their advertisers to convince women that they should see themselves as consumers first and foremost and that they could construct their identities and the social status of their families through consumption (Figure 1.7).
In spite of the successful eforts made by manufacturing industry to introduce modern styles into the domestic arena, the home also retained its traditional face. at was especially apparent in the living room, the site of family values whi depended on memory for their continuity.
It frequently featured a wide range of material references to the past and inherited objects also had an important role to fulfil.
Nonetheless, several signs of an encroaing modernity were also in evidence. e claustrophobic Victorian parlour was gradually being replaced by lighter, brighter living rooms containing fewer pieces of furniture and less cluer. at cautious, evolutionary approa to an acceptance of a transformed environment has been described by the literary historian, Alison Light, as being aracteristic of a preference for a ‘conservative modernity’.
A number of historians have demonstrated that the path to modernity was not the same for everyone and that factors su as gender, class, age and ethnicity afected the ways in whi it was negotiated.
Most women engaged with some level of modernity in the inter-war years, however.
In all but the most afluent of homes, interior decoration was undertaken by housewives themselves, facilitated by consumption and tradesmen.
Examples of modern interior semes were available to them in magazines, shop windows and films – Hollywood films played a key role influencing both interior décor and dress – providing important sources of inspiration.<sup>51</sup> Nowhere was the adoption of modern styles disseminated through the mass media more apparent than in the new suburban residential areas that were developed around the key cities in the USA and Europe.
Inhabited for the most part by the new upper working class and middle class, those areas developed an alternative to sophisticated metropolitan taste. e social and cultural geography that separated the city from the suburb was recognizable across the industrialized world, albeit with inevitable local variations. e suburbs engaged with what the cities had consumed some time earlier and had subsequently rejected as being unfashionable.
In addition, the suburb and the city supported completely diferent systems of production and consumption that remained separated from ea other.
It became apparent that it was possible for distinctive taste cultures – all of them modern according to their own definition of the term – to coexist in close proximity to ea other.
Ben Fine and Ellen Leopold’s theory of ‘systems of provision was developed to explain that phenomenon. ey have maintained that people’s tastes are formed in the economic context of the particular system of provision available to them.
However, they ignored the role played by the globally disseminated media, to whi most people had access in the inter-war years and whi were as efective as agents of taste formation as what was available in the local shops.
By the late 1930s, a concept of modernity had penetrated the lives of many people living in the western industrialized world. e efects of marketing, advertising and branding were widespread and there was a high level of awareness about the stylistic languages of mass-produced goods, images and spaces.
Self and group identities were increasingly being negotiated through consumption and it had become clear that, while meanings were injected into images and objects at the moment of their production, they were supplemented and transformed as they entered into everyday life.
In turn, new desires were stimulated that required the creation of new goods and images to satisfy them. us was the fashion cycle fuelled.
Consuming modern visual, material and spatial culture, and thereby embracing modernity, had, by the inter-war years, become one of the primary means through whi the majority of the inhabitants of the industrialized world acquired their identities and positioned themselves in society.
Su was the intensification and expansion of that process that designers found themselves at the very epicentre of modern consumer culture.

## 2 The
impact of technology

## New
production methods, new materials Mass production is the focussing upon a manufacturing project of the principles of power, accuracy, economy, system, continuity, and speed.
In the first four decades of the twentieth century, one face of modern design was defined by its sociocultural role within the expanding picture of consumption and the newly defined marketplace – an area of modern life that could not be easily rationalized and systematized.
Its other important defining context was the more rationally based world of mass manufacturing and tenological innovation.
In that context, large numbers of standardized goods were produced to meet expanding demand but, because of the high investment costs required, they had to be aggressively sold.
Design crossed the tenology/culture divide.
A process that was intrinsic to mass manufacturing, the results of whi communicated sociocultural values, it was embedded equally firmly within the worlds of consumption and production.
Arguably, it was one of the few phenomena that linked those two worlds together.
Tenological innovation made possible the manufacture of vast numbers of newly conceived products that entered the marketplace from the late nineteenth century onwards.
As well as constituting a whole new area of visual and material culture, complementing the more traditional decorative arts, the new goods also allenged and excited designers’ imaginations. e wide availability of su novel objects as vacuum cleaners, electrical appliances and new modes of transportation, as well as the new forms of advertising and retail display developed to promote their sales in those years, provided a primary means through whi consumers could create new identities for themselves and negotiate their entry into modern life. e modern concept of design, whi moved beyond the traditional decorative arts and the cra process that had generated them, was a direct consequence of a defining feature of industrial manufacturing – the division of labour. e new means of producing goods caused the work of the traditional crasmen to become fragmented into a number of new, diferentiated tasks, one of whi – the initial conceptualization of the product – required the development of a new process.
From the outset, designers had a twofold responsibility.
As well as conceiving new products and planning their manufacture, they also had to work in the context of consumption to ensure that their products contained appropriate sociocultural meanings.
Designers were uniquely placed, therefore, to express modernity in the contexts of both production and consumption.
Industrialization was one of the key defining phenomena of the modern world.
In Britain, the discovery of steam power inspired the invention of numerous new maine tools and production teniques.
In turn, those new tools facilitated the manufacture of new consumer maines.
New materials, cast iron among them, were responsible for dramatic anges in the design of products and the environment, making possible new forms of decoration. ose discoveries transformed the production of the traditional decorative arts.
In textile production, for example, the Spinning Jenny and the Jacquard loom revolutionized the ways in whi textiles were both conceived and made, requiring designers to make decisions in advance of manufacture, unlike cra workers, who had been able to make aesthetic and material oices as they went along. at seemingly simple modification represented an important shi from the working process of the crasman, who depended upon tacit skills, to that of the designer, who needed to engage in rational planning.
It brought with it radical anges in many manufacturing sectors, among them fabric printing ceramics production.
While Britain witnessed transformations in the manufacturing of the traditional decorative art industries, textiles, ceramics, glass and furniture among them, the USA made huge advances in the eficient organization of labour and production in the new, tenologically oriented industries, in the development of highly specialized maine tools and in the manufacture of new goods with a technological bias.
Much has been written about what was dubbed, ‘the American System of Manufacture’.
H.
J.
Habakkuk, for example, has described the developments in both Britain and the USA, explaining that mechanization was much more advanced in the latter country because, given the greater availability of farming land, manual labour was in short supply.
Siegfried Giedion’s seminal text, <sub>Mechanisation</sub> <sub>Takes</sub> <sub>Command</sub> of 1948, focused on the importance of the maine to American culture and to that country’s pre-eminence in modern, functional design.
Indeed, modernism – the dominant design movement of the mid twentieth-century – considered the maine, with its links function and rationality, as the key metaphor and the source of aesthetic inspiration for modern designers. <sub>Figure 2.1</sub> Henry Ford’s ‘Model T’ production line at the Highland Park factory, 1913 (from the collections of Henry Ford Museum and Greenfield Village) <sup>David A.
Hounshell’s book,</sup> From the American System to Mass Production 1800–1932: The Development of Manufacturing Technology in the <sub>US</sub>, has focused on the fundamental continuities within the story of American manufacturing in this period.
He has outlined a story that began with the anges that took place in American arms production in the nineteenth century and moved through to automobile manufacturing in the 1920s.
His narrative has taken in clos, sewing maines, bicycles, agricultural mainery and automobiles along the way and he has maintained that ea industry learnt from the one before it, and that continuity was provided through the use of steel as a basic material, the development of standardized components and the use of specialist maine tools.
